November 9, 2026 to February 7, 2027

Content by brand

One tab per brand. For K&G Market and Klean NYC, “Calendar” is the 3-month plan, and “Overview” and “Week 1” to “Week 4” are the 4 weeks of posts ready to copy, with scripts, prompts and TikToks. JDET Capital and Klean Peru have their calendar.

Summary

Phases, weeks, reviews, adjustment rules and key dates for the four brands.

How it is built

The first four weeks are detailed day by day because there is already starting data. The other nine are planned by topic. At each review Metricool is checked, compared against the baseline, and the next block is detailed. Planning all 13 weeks piece by piece would get ahead of data that doesn't exist yet.

Base · weeks 1 to 4

November 9 to December 6. Set the pace, organize each brand's identity and measure. Includes Thanksgiving.

Season · weeks 5 to 8

December 7 to January 3. Christmas, year-end and New Year, with one low week.

Consolidation · weeks 9 to 13

January 4 to February 7. Repeat what worked and prepare the next quarter.

Review points

DateWhat is reviewedWhat is decided
Fri Nov 20Pace compliance, impressions per post and first readings on format and time.Adjust times. Keep the plan or drop to the minimum.
Fri Dec 4Four weeks against the baseline: followers, impressions, Reel, Google clicks and LinkedIn.Result of the Instagram window, formats to repeat and the detail of weeks 5 to 8.
Fri Dec 18Holiday season: reach and messages.January detail (weeks 9 to 12) and the pace of the low week.
Fri Jan 29Full quarter against the proposed targets.February to April plan.

On each date it is enough to ask for the review: Metricool data is checked and this calendar is updated.

Adjustment rules

Proposed thresholds, to confirm with the first data.

  • K&G Market Reel. If it exceeds 150 views two weeks in a row, go from one Reel to two per week.
  • Klean N Green NYC Reels. At review 1 they are compared with the Wednesday carousels. If they have less reach, Friday becomes a single image.
  • Instagram time. If the 6 PM to 8 PM window beats the 10 AM to 12 PM window in impressions for two weeks, move Instagram to the evening.
  • LinkedIn doing well. If a post exceeds 50 impressions, repeat the topic in another format over the next two weeks.
  • LinkedIn with low reach. If it stays under 15 impressions per post after four weeks, strengthen connections and shares from personal profiles before increasing the pace.
  • TikTok. The accounts are new and there is no data of their own: the Tuesday and Thursday 6 PM times come from Metricool's general reference. At review 1, views and full plays are compared between Tuesday and Thursday and between topics; the type of topic that beats the others is repeated and the time is moved if another slot performs better.
  • Team capacity. If less than 80% of what is scheduled is met two weeks in a row, go back to the minimum plan.

Peruvian and Caribbean products in Brooklyn.

Products in brackets are confirmed with the store. Ají amarillo and chicha morada already appear in the account's posts.

Overview

Texts, image prompts, Reels and hashtags for K&G Market over four weeks.

What is ready and what is missing

K&G Market, Monday, November 9 to Sunday, December 6, 2026. All times are New York time.

Next step. All texts are written in English with a Spanish line, as the brand voice defines. What is left is the real material: opening hours, the products behind each bracket, the team photo, and the Saturday Stories on the day. Nothing is published without approval.

Characters: Rosa and Dre

Two recurring AI characters, with their fixed look, voice and reference prompts.

The idea. Rosa and Dre are the two recurring AI characters of K&G Market. Each has a fixed look, voice and way of speaking, so viewers learn to recognize them from one video or image to the next. They are hosts that share tips and invite the viewer. They are not employees, customers or testimonials, and every piece that shows them is labeled as AI.

2recurring characters: Rosa and DreData
19appearances planned across videos, Reels and imagesData
1reference portrait per character, generated once and attached to every video and image promptReference

Create each character once

  • 1. Portrait. Paste the portrait prompt of each character in your image generator and keep generating until the face matches the description. Save the best one as the reference.
  • 2. Sheet (optional). Generate the three-view sheet from the portrait. It helps when a tool drifts between front and side views.
  • 3. Voice. Paste the voice description in ElevenLabs Voice Design, save the voice you like and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of that character.
  • 4. Attach. Every video and image prompt that shows a character already carries its fixed description and asks for the reference image. Attach the portrait each time.
  • 5. Check. Compare each result with the portrait: face, hair, skin tone, age and accessories. If it drifted, regenerate instead of fixing it by hand.
Rosa · Pantry and produce guide
  • Role: Pantry and produce guide.
  • Look (fixed): Rosa, a woman in her 60s with short silver hair, warm brown skin, soft laugh lines and small gold hoop earrings, wearing a denim apron over a cream blouse.
  • Personality: Patient, warm and a little playful. Explains a product the way a neighbor would: slowly, with one clear tip.
  • How she speaks: Short, calm sentences in clear English. Product and dish names keep their own names, and a short Spanish phrase is welcome. Starts from the product, not from herself.
  • Topics: Peruvian pantry (ají amarillo, yuca, purple corn), how to choose produce, the holiday table.
  • First line, first video only: "Hi, I'm Rosa."
  • Appears in: 4 images, 2 Reels, 4 TikToks.
Rosa: reference portrait prompt (generate first)
Photorealistic portrait photograph of Rosa, a woman in her 60s with short silver hair, warm brown skin, soft laugh lines and small gold hoop earrings, wearing a denim apron over a cream blouse. Front-facing, chest-up, relaxed expression with a hint of a smile, looking straight at the camera. Soft diffuse daylight, plain light-gray wall, shallow depth of field, natural skin texture with fine lines and pores, true-to-life colors. Vertical 4:5 format, 1080 x 1350 px. No text, no logos, no watermark, no extra jewelry beyond what is described. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Rosa: three-view sheet prompt (optional)
Character reference sheet of Rosa, a woman in her 60s with short silver hair, warm brown skin, soft laugh lines and small gold hoop earrings, wearing a denim apron over a cream blouse. The same person shown three times side by side on a plain light-gray background: front view, three-quarter view and side profile, all chest-up, with the same relaxed expression and identical hair, clothing and accessories in the three views. Photorealistic, soft even daylight, natural skin texture. Horizontal 3:2 format. No text, no labels, no watermark. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Rosa: voice description for ElevenLabs Voice Design
A warm, unhurried woman's voice in her 60s. Clear American English at a relaxed pace, with natural, gentle pronunciation of Spanish words. Friendly and reassuring, like a neighbor explaining a recipe.

Dre · Caribbean kitchen guide
  • Role: Caribbean kitchen guide.
  • Look (fixed): Dre, a man in his early 30s with a short trimmed beard, light brown skin, short black hair and a thin silver chain, wearing a black T-shirt.
  • Personality: Upbeat, quick and practical. Gives the tip first and the reason second, like a friend leaning over the counter.
  • How he speaks: Short, punchy sentences in everyday English. Names the product and the dish in the first five words. Dish names keep their own names (tostones, maduros, scotch bonnet).
  • Topics: Caribbean cooking (plantains, rice and peas, scotch bonnet), mangoes and pineapples, quick kitchen tips.
  • First line, first video only: "Hi, I'm Dre."
  • Appears in: 3 images, 2 Reels, 4 TikToks.
Dre: reference portrait prompt (generate first)
Photorealistic portrait photograph of Dre, a man in his early 30s with a short trimmed beard, light brown skin, short black hair and a thin silver chain, wearing a black T-shirt. Front-facing, chest-up, relaxed expression with a hint of a smile, looking straight at the camera. Soft diffuse daylight, plain light-gray wall, shallow depth of field, natural skin texture with fine lines and pores, true-to-life colors. Vertical 4:5 format, 1080 x 1350 px. No text, no logos, no watermark, no extra jewelry beyond what is described. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Dre: three-view sheet prompt (optional)
Character reference sheet of Dre, a man in his early 30s with a short trimmed beard, light brown skin, short black hair and a thin silver chain, wearing a black T-shirt. The same person shown three times side by side on a plain light-gray background: front view, three-quarter view and side profile, all chest-up, with the same relaxed expression and identical hair, clothing and accessories in the three views. Photorealistic, soft even daylight, natural skin texture. Horizontal 3:2 format. No text, no labels, no watermark. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Dre: voice description for ElevenLabs Voice Design
A friendly, upbeat man's voice in his early 30s. Clear American English at an easy, quick pace, with light Caribbean warmth and a smiling tone. Sounds like a friend giving a quick tip.

Rules

  • Where they appear: the TikToks, the four Wednesday Reels and five single images (Nov 9, Nov 16, Nov 20, Nov 30 and Dec 4).
  • Where they never appear: the Google Business Profile posts, the Saturday Stories, the team photo post (Nov 13) and any photo of the store itself. Google prefers real photos of the business, and the team post must show the real team.
  • Real products: a character can hold or present a product, but the photo or video of the shelf, the price and the stock come from the store.
  • Labels. Videos end the caption with "AI-generated video." and use TikTok's AI label. Images that show Rosa and Dre need the platform's AI label if the app offers one, or the line "Image made with AI." at the end of the caption.
  • No testimonials, no claims. The characters never say they bought, cooked or cleaned anything themselves, and the captions never speak in their name.
  • No likeness. The descriptions are fictional. If a generated face resembles a real person you know of, regenerate it.

Pieces and sizes

PieceSizeNotes
Photo and single image1080 x 1350 px (4:5)Instagram and Facebook. Five of them (Nov 9, 16, 20, 30 and Dec 4) show Rosa or Dre as the main image; a real photo or a product-only image is the alternative. No text on the generated image: the key details are added in Canva.
Carousel (Nov 23)1080 x 1350 px (4:5)3 images, one product per image.
Reel1080 x 1920 px (9:16)About 12 seconds, 3 Seedance scenes of about 4 seconds, with Rosa or Dre cooking: the face shows in two scenes and only the hands in the third. Wednesdays, Instagram and Facebook.
Stories1080 x 1920 px (9:16)Saturdays, 3 frames shot that day with the phone. Keep key content out of the top and bottom 250 px.
Google Business Profile1200 x 900 px (4:3)Mondays. A real photo is better; the generated image is the alternative. No phone number in the text.

Look of the images

  • Photorealistic, soft daylight with a warm tone, wood, linen and white ceramic, so the produce color carries the image.
  • No text in generated images; text goes on in Canva. Rosa and Dre, the two AI characters, are the only people allowed in generated images and videos. Real people appear only in real photos, with their permission.
  • Bottom-left corner empty for the logo, as in the Klean N Green NYC pack.
  • Stories are not generated: they are shot that day with the phone.

Video prompts for Seedance

Every Reel in this pack follows the same settings.

  • Aspect ratio 9:16 (vertical), the highest resolution the tool offers (1080 x 1920 px or above).
  • 4 seconds per scene, 3 scenes, about 12 seconds in total. Use 5 seconds per scene if you want more room to trim when editing.
  • Sound: ambient only (pouring, sizzling, simmering). No music and no voice inside Seedance; add the voiceover (each Reel card has its script) and licensed music in the video editor.
  • Paste the continuity block at the start of every scene prompt, so the kitchen, the light, the props and the hands stay the same from scene to scene.
  • Generate and judge one scene at a time. If the tool offers it, use the last frame of the previous scene as the reference for the next one.
  • Change one line at a time: if the movement is right but the framing is off, edit only the Camera line; if the framing is right but the movement is off, edit only Subject and action.
  • Every Reel here has a cook, Rosa or Dre. The constraints line allows that one character and nobody else; attach the character's reference portrait to every scene prompt.

Before approving

  • Opening hours. Every text has [opening hours]. Send the regular hours and the special ones for Nov 26, Nov 27, Dec 24, Dec 25, Dec 31 and Jan 1.
  • Products. The brackets for the Caribbean product (Nov 16), the three Thanksgiving products (Nov 23) and the restocked product (Nov 30) need real items. Ají amarillo and chicha morada already appear in the account's own posts.
  • Dishes in the Reels. Rice and peas (Nov 18) and sweet plantains (Nov 25) are proposals for the [Caribbean dish] and [dish] in the calendar. Confirm that the store sells the ingredients, or give another dish and the prompts are rewritten.
  • Purple corn (Dec 2). The Reel assumes the store sells purple corn. If it sells ready-made chicha morada, the steps change to "shake, chill and pour".
  • Pre-orders. Thanksgiving and Christmas posts mention [date, if offered]. If the store does not take orders ahead, delete those lines.
  • Store hours. The website header lists Mon to Sat 7:30am to 8pm and Sun 7:30am to 6:30pm. The [hours] in the holiday posts (Thanksgiving, Small Business Saturday, December) stay open because holiday hours may differ: send the real ones and they are filled in.
  • Phone number. No text includes it. The website and the Google profile show +1 347-365-9656 and a Facebook extract shows 347-739-5457: confirm which one is correct before adding it anywhere.
  • Website. The Google button points to kngmarket.com. The site was hidden from Google by a WordPress setting; on Nov 9 that was turned off, the sitemap was sent to Search Console and indexing of the home page was requested. It can take from a few days to a couple of weeks to show up. The site now has Yoast SEO with titles and descriptions on the main pages and every product category, one H1 per page and alt text on the product images. If you prefer, use the Call now button once the phone is confirmed.
  • Hashtag #KGMarket. Check on Instagram that no other business uses it. The Small Business Saturday and Christmas posts add #SmallBusinessSaturday and #ChristmasFood to the calendar's sets.
  • Team photo (Nov 13). Needs the team's permission and their names. Never use a generated image for that post.
  • Rosa and Dre. They are AI characters, not staff, and they appear only in the TikToks, the four Reels and five single images. Generate each portrait once (see Characters) and attach it every time. The AI label is needed on every video and image that shows them. The Google posts, the Stories and the team post stay free of generated people.
  • Spanish lines. They are written in neutral Spanish. Tell me if the store prefers Peruvian or Caribbean terms for any product.
  • Colors and logo. No palette or fonts were provided for K&G Market, so the prompts use natural food colors and leave the bottom-left corner empty for your logo. If you share the logo and colors, the Canva overlays can match.
  • Metricool. The 36 drafts (28 for Instagram, Facebook and Google, plus 8 for TikTok) are created for K&G Market and none is published. Before scheduling each one, in the editor: replace the Instagram placeholder image with the real one, fill in every bracket, switch the four Instagram and Facebook Reels to Reel and upload the video, add the Brooklyn location on Instagram and Facebook, add the image and button on the Google posts, and on TikTok replace the placeholder photo with the video and switch on the AI-generated content label.

TikTok with recurring characters

Posting times come from Metricool's general TikTok reference, because the account is new and has no history yet.

Format. Eight TikToks for K&G Market (@kngmarket), two per week, Tuesday and Thursday at 6 PM New York time (week 3 moves for Thanksgiving). Each one is a video of about 18 seconds in which one of the brand's two recurring AI characters, Rosa and Dre, talks to the camera. The topics are product and produce tips: how to choose, how to use, what to cook. The scripts, prompts and captions are inside each week, among the other pieces. The characters, their reference portraits and their rules are in the Characters section above.

8TikToks for K&G Market, each with its script, video prompt and caption: 4 with Rosa, 4 with DreData
8drafts saved in Metricool, with a placeholder photo until you add the videoData
18 sabout, per video: 3 beats of 36 to 47 wordsData

TikTok: how each video is made

  • 1. Generate. Copy the video prompt in Seedance or a similar tool. If it does not produce a voice with accurate lip sync, record the script in ElevenLabs and apply lip sync to the video.
  • 2. Clips. If your generator caps a clip below 18 seconds, generate beats 1 and 2 as one clip and beat 3 as another, with the same presenter and setting, and join them in the editor.
  • 3. Check. The face, hands and mouth look natural, the words match the script, and nothing on screen is garbled. Regenerate otherwise.
  • 4. Edit. Add the on-screen text and subtitles in the TikTok or video editor. Generators garble text inside video, so the prompts ask for none.
  • 5. Upload. Open the Metricool draft, remove the placeholder photo, upload the video and switch on the AI-generated content label.
  • 6. Schedule. Only after you have checked it. Nothing here is published without your approval.

TikTok: character rules

  • Two recurring characters. Rosa and Dre are the only presenters. The same face, hair, skin tone and clothes in every video, so viewers recognize them; the settings change from video to video. The look is fixed in the Characters section and repeated word for word in every prompt.
  • Reference image first. Generate each character's portrait once, keep the best one and attach it to every video prompt. Check each result against the portrait before using it; regenerate if the face or hair drifted.
  • The character is a host, not a customer or an employee. The scripts give tips and information. None says "I shop here" or "I have been cleaning for years", because an invented person cannot give a real testimonial or speak as staff.
  • Always labeled as AI. Every caption ends with "AI-generated video." and the TikTok AI-generated content label must be on when the video is uploaded. Each character introduces themself by name in the first video only.
  • Avoid in every prompt: subtitles, logos, watermarks, music, extra people, readable brand names and text on screen. The characters never resemble a real person.

TikTok: before approving

  • Products. Every video mentions products the store should have on that date. Confirm stock before scheduling, or change the line. The scripts say "ask us" where stock is not certain.
  • Hours. The captions of Nov 24 and Nov 25 contain [hours]. The website lists Mon to Sat 7:30am to 8pm and Sun 7:30am to 6:30pm: confirm those apply that week, then fill them in or delete the line.
  • Thursday, November 26. It is Thanksgiving, so there is no TikTok that day. Week 3 moves to Tuesday and Wednesday.
  • Facts. The produce tips are general and common (ripeness, storage, safe cooking of yuca). If the store prefers different advice, change the script.
  • Spanish. The scripts are in English, with one short Spanish line in the first video. The captions carry the Spanish. Tell me if the store wants more Spanish in the voice.
  • Where Rosa and Dre appear. In the TikToks, the four Reels and five single images. Google posts, Stories and the team photo stay free of generated people.
  • Commercial content label. Metricool left TikTok's "your brand" option off. Decide when scheduling whether to turn it on.

On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: 3 ways to use ají amarillo · 1. Huancaína
Scene 2: 2. Ají de gallina
Scene 3: 3. Ceviche
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 23 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Ají amarillo, three ways. One: huancaína over potatoes.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Two: ají de gallina, creamy and warm.
8-12 s  Scene 3: Three: ceviche! Find the ají at K&G Market.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Rosa's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Rosa.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[curious] Ají amarillo, three ways. [warmly] One: huancaína over potatoes.
[warmly] Two: ají de gallina, creamy and warm.
[excited] Three: ceviche! Find the ají at K&G Market.

TikTok: Plantains: green, yellow or black?

TikTok @kngmarket · AI video, about 18 s Dre Draft · add video

Script, on-screen text and video prompt

Reel: Rice and peas in 3 steps

Instagram and Facebook · Reel, about 12 seconds Text ready · add video

Metricool: status
  • Drafts created in Metricool for K&G Market (Instagram and Facebook), New York time, with the caption and hashtags. Nothing is published. Instagram holds a placeholder image and Facebook is text only: in the editor, switch each one to Reel and upload the video.
  • Reels are the format that reached the most people on this account (the August Reel got 136 views), so this is the weekly anchor.
What the video needs
  • Vertical 9:16, 1080 x 1920 px. 3 scenes of 4 seconds each, about 12 seconds in total.
  • If the store can film the real dish, a real video beats a generated one. The prompts below are for when it cannot.
  • Dre is the cook in this Reel (scenes 1 and 3 show the face; scene 2 shows the hands). Attach Dre's reference portrait to every scene prompt. The voiceover is Dre's voice; the voice description is in the Characters section of the Overview.
  • Dre is an AI character. Switch on the AI label when you schedule this Reel.
  • Add the on-screen text, the voiceover (script below) and licensed music in the video editor: generators garble text inside video.
  • Cover: use a frame from scene 1 and add the title in Canva.
  • The settings and the way the prompts are built are explained in the Overview, under "Video prompts for Seedance".

On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Rice and peas in 3 steps · 1. Simmer the beans
Scene 2: 2. Add the rice and one whole scotch bonnet
Scene 3: 3. Cover, steam, fluff
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 28 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Rice and peas in three steps. One: simmer the beans.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Two: add the rice and one whole scotch bonnet.
8-12 s  Scene 3: Three: cover, steam, fluff. It's all at K&G Market!
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Dre's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Dre.

[curious] Rice and peas in three steps. One: simmer the beans.
[warmly] Two: add the rice and one whole scotch bonnet.
[excited] Three: cover, steam, fluff. It's all at K&G Market!

What the video needs

On-screen text by scene
On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Sweet plantains for Thanksgiving · 1. Slice
Scene 2: 2. Fry until golden
Scene 3: 3. Serve warm
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 23 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Sweet plantains for your Thanksgiving table. One: slice ripe plantains.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Two: fry until deep golden.
8-12 s  Scene 3: Three: serve warm! Get them at K&G Market.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Dre's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Dre.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[curious] Sweet plantains for your Thanksgiving table. One: slice ripe plantains.
[warmly] Two: fry until deep golden.
[excited] Three: serve warm! Get them at K&G Market.
Plain script, without tags, to record yourself

  • Reels are the format that reached the most people on this account (the August Reel got 136 views), so this is the weekly anchor.
What the video needs
  • Vertical 9:16, 1080 x 1920 px. 3 scenes of 4 seconds each, about 12 seconds in total.
  • If the store can film the real dish, a real video beats a generated one. The prompts below are for when it cannot.
  • Rosa is the cook in this Reel (scenes 1 and 3 show the face; scene 2 shows the hands). Attach Rosa's reference portrait to every scene prompt. The voiceover is Rosa's voice; the voice description is in the Characters section of the Overview.

On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Chicha morada at home · 1. Simmer
Scene 2: 2. Strain, add sugar and lime
Scene 3: 3. Chill and pour over ice
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 28 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Chicha morada, step by step. One: simmer purple corn with spices.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Two: strain it, then add sugar and lime.
8-12 s  Scene 3: Three: serve over ice! Purple corn at K&G Market.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Rosa's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Rosa.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[curious] Chicha morada, step by step. One: simmer purple corn with spices.
[warmly] Two: strain it, then add sugar and lime.
[excited] Three: serve over ice! Purple corn at K&G Market.

5 things to start Peruvian + Caribbean cooking
Video prompt (Seedance or similar)

Dre (a man in his early 30s with a short trimmed beard, light brown skin, short black hair and a thin silver chain, wearing a black T-shirt) speaks directly to the camera in a kitchen table with five ingredients laid out on a linen cloth. Use the attached reference image of Dre for the face, hair, build and clothing; if the tool does not accept a reference image, follow this description exactly. Natural, friendly, conversational delivery with small hand gestures, chest-up handheld selfie framing, soft natural light, realistic skin and hair, accurate lip sync. Take this list to K&G Market and ask us." No on-screen text, no subtitles, no logos, no watermark, no music. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.

Klean N Green NYC

Cleaning services for homes, offices and property management in New York.

Content strategy: time back

Core message. Hiring Klean N Green NYC gives people time to do what they couldn't before. Each piece either shows what the person can do with that time (about 2 in 3) or is a useful tip that ends with a bridge to the service (1 in 3). None promises hours saved or tells what real clients did with their time.

25 of 38pieces show the time back (the mix is about 2 in 3)Data
13 of 38pieces stay as tips and close with a bridge to the serviceData
15pieces already scheduled with their media: only the text changesData
23drafts: new text and, where the idea changed, new scenes, scripts and promptsData

Approval. Nothing in Metricool has changed. When you approve each piece, it is updated: scheduled posts are edited in place (they keep their time and media) and drafts are replaced with the new text. A piece that is not approved stays as it is.

Weeks 1 to 4: day by day (New York time)

Pace. Full 7-day plan: Monday Google, Tuesday LinkedIn, Wednesday Instagram and Facebook (carousel), Thursday LinkedIn, Friday Reel, Saturday single image on Instagram and Facebook, Sunday Instagram. On top of that, two TikToks per week at 6 PM (Tuesday and Thursday; in week 3, Tuesday and Friday) and two LinkedIn videos, on Wednesday, November 25 and December 2 at 11 AM. Maya and Daniel, the two recurring AI characters, host the TikToks from Nov 12 and the LinkedIn videos, and appear in the Reels of Nov 27, Dec 4 and Dec 6 and in the single images of Nov 28, Nov 29 and Dec 5. Weeks 1 and 2 are already scheduled with their media and keep it; weeks 3 and 4 are drafts.

Blog on kleannyc.com

Proposal. The site's blog exists but has stalled. It restarts with one article every two weeks, each on a single concrete search and with a closing button to request an estimate. LinkedIn posts link to the article instead of the contact page when there is a related one. The effect on Google takes months and is not guaranteed.

The LinkedIn drafts for November 24 and December 3 currently point to the contact page with UTM parameters. If the article is published in time, the link is changed; if not, they stay as they are.

  • Site status (Nov 9). Done: /dtut/ and /new-clients/ are now noindex, so they no longer compete with the main pages. The robots.txt lines without a User-agent are ignored by Google and block nothing, so they were left as they are. Every service page has its own title, description and a single H1, and the old addresses (/air-bnb/, /commercial-cleaning-nyc/, /residential-cleaning-nyc/, /post-construction-cleaning-nyc/) redirect with a 301. Still open: /post-renovation-cleaning/ repeats the full text of /post-construction-cleaning/. Decide whether to rewrite one, redirect one or mark one as canonical before linking articles to either.
  • Each article. A single question, a clear title, a featured image and the closing button to request an estimate with UTM parameters.

Overview

Image prompts, texts and hashtags for Klean N Green NYC over four weeks.

Strategy: time back

The mix: about two pieces in three show the time, and one in three teaches and bridges.

The idea. Hiring Klean N Green NYC gives you time for what you could not fit in before. Every piece either shows what that time can become, or gives a useful tip and ends with a bridge to the service.

25pieces show what you can do with the time: a Saturday, a host's evening, a property manager's weekData
13pieces stay as tips and close with a bridge to the serviceData
15pieces are already scheduled with finished images or videos (weeks 1 and 2, plus the Nov 10 TikTok): only their text changesData
23pieces are drafts: new text, and new scenes, scripts and prompts where the idea changedData
AudienceWhat the time is forWhere it shows up
Families and homesWeekends, a slow breakfast, a walk, the call you keep postponingSaturday and Sunday posts, Reels, Google
HostsThe door, the drinks and the guests, not the spongeNov 18, Nov 27, Nov 28, TikTok
Rental hosts and landlordsGuest messages, showings and the listingGoogle, Nov 16
Property managersTenants, showings and inspections, instead of coordinating crewsLinkedIn: Nov 10, Nov 17, Dec 1, Dec 3
Office managers and teamsPeople, vendors, projects and the year-end closeLinkedIn: Nov 12 and Nov 24; Google, Nov 23
  • Lead with the moment, then the service. Open with what the person gets: a Saturday, a quiet evening, an open calendar. The service is how they get there.
  • Ask, do not claim. "What would you do with your Saturday?" invites the reader. The copy never says what clients did with their time: no invented clients, stories or testimonials. The only client voice is the Nov 19 Google review, word for word.
  • No hours-saved numbers. The numbers that remain (5 checks, 20 minutes, 30 minutes) are steps inside tips, not promises.
  • Tips stay, with a bridge. One piece in three is a tip. It ends with one bridge line from the bank below.
  • One call to action per piece: a free estimate, a message or a scope.
  • Scenes show the free time, not the work. Maya, Daniel and Clover appear after the work is done: reading, a mug at the window, waiting for guests.
  • Weeks 1 and 2 keep their media. Those posts are scheduled with finished images and videos, so the proposal changes only the text. Tip carousels and Reels carry the bridge in the caption, and three carousels have an optional extra closing slide.
Bridge lines for tips (pick one per piece)
Prefer to spend that time elsewhere? We handle it. Message us for a free estimate.
Save the list, or hand it to us and keep the time.
Or let us take it off your list.
Want the whole day back? Message us for a free estimate.

What is ready and what is missing

Characters: Maya and Daniel

Two recurring AI characters, with their fixed look, voice and reference prompts.

The idea. Maya and Daniel are the two recurring AI characters of Klean N Green NYC. Each has a fixed look, voice and way of speaking, so viewers learn to recognize them from one video or image to the next. They are hosts that share tips and invite the viewer. They are not employees, customers or testimonials, and every piece that shows them is labeled as AI. Clover, the gray tabby cat, stays as the non-human character in the single images.

2recurring characters: Maya and DanielData
18appearances planned across videos, Reels and imagesData
1reference portrait per character, generated once and attached to every video and image promptReference

Create each character once

  • 1. Portrait. Paste the portrait prompt of each character in your image generator and keep generating until the face matches the description. Save the best one as the reference.
  • 2. Sheet (optional). Generate the three-view sheet from the portrait. It helps when a tool drifts between front and side views.
  • 3. Voice. Paste the voice description in ElevenLabs Voice Design, save the voice you like and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of that character.
  • 4. Attach. Every video and image prompt that shows a character already carries its fixed description and asks for the reference image. Attach the portrait each time.
  • 5. Check. Compare each result with the portrait: face, hair, skin tone, age and accessories. If it drifted, regenerate instead of fixing it by hand.
Maya · Time-back host
  • Role: Time-back host.
  • Look (fixed): Maya, a woman in her early 30s with warm tan skin, dark brown wavy hair in a loose low bun and small silver stud earrings, wearing a soft sage-green sweater.
  • Personality: Calm, bright and easygoing. Talks about the free time first and the cleaning second, and never lectures.
  • How she speaks: Opens with a question or the moment, not with a claim. Sentences of about ten words. Ends by handing the day back to the viewer.
  • Topics: Homes and apartments: what to do with the time you get back, cleaning order and quick tips, hosting.
  • First line, first video only: "Hi, I'm Maya."
  • Appears in: 1 Instagram carousel (hands), 2 Reels, 2 single images, 4 TikToks.
Maya: reference portrait prompt (generate first)
Photorealistic portrait photograph of Maya, a woman in her early 30s with warm tan skin, dark brown wavy hair in a loose low bun and small silver stud earrings, wearing a soft sage-green sweater. Front-facing, chest-up, relaxed expression with a hint of a smile, looking straight at the camera. Soft diffuse daylight, plain light-gray wall, shallow depth of field, natural skin texture with fine lines and pores, true-to-life colors. Vertical 4:5 format, 1080 x 1350 px. No text, no logos, no watermark, no extra jewelry beyond what is described. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Maya: three-view sheet prompt (optional)
Character reference sheet of Maya, a woman in her early 30s with warm tan skin, dark brown wavy hair in a loose low bun and small silver stud earrings, wearing a soft sage-green sweater. The same person shown three times side by side on a plain light-gray background: front view, three-quarter view and side profile, all chest-up, with the same relaxed expression and identical hair, clothing and accessories in the three views. Photorealistic, soft even daylight, natural skin texture. Horizontal 3:2 format. No text, no labels, no watermark. A realistic fictional person, not the likeness of any real person.
Maya: voice description for ElevenLabs Voice Design
A calm, bright woman's voice in her early 30s. Clear American English, relaxed and conversational, with a smiling tone, like a friend inviting you to take your weekend back.

Daniel · Property and office host
  • Role: Property and office host.
  • Look (fixed): Daniel, a man in his late 40s with light brown skin, short dark hair and a trimmed salt-and-pepper beard, wearing a navy quarter-zip sweater over a white collared shirt.
  • Personality: Steady, practical and reassuring. Explains a checklist the way a property manager would: in order, with no drama.
  • How he speaks: Numbered steps and plain words. Names the situation first (turnover, vacant unit, office), then the steps. Never says he cleans or manages anything himself.
  • Topics: Property managers and office teams: turnovers, vacant units, what to ask a provider, what to send before booking.
  • First line, first video only: "Hi, I'm Daniel."
  • Appears in: 2 LinkedIn carousels (hands), 1 single image, 1 Reel, 3 TikToks, 2 LinkedIn videos.

Rules

  • Where they appear: the TikToks from Nov 12, the two LinkedIn videos, the Reels of Nov 27, Dec 4 and Dec 6, the single images of Nov 28, Nov 29 and Dec 5, and as hands in three carousels (After dinner, the office checklist and the property management carousel). Clover appears in single images, as before.
  • Where they never appear: the weeks 1 and 2 posts, which are scheduled with finished media; the Nov 10 TikTok, which is scheduled with a finished video; the Dec 2 before and after (real photos); the Google posts; and the images of the website pages.
  • What the scenes show: in time-back pieces Maya and Daniel appear after the work is done (a mug at the window, a book on the sofa, a checklist already ticked). In tip pieces they can demonstrate the tip. They are never shown cleaning a client's home.
  • Labels. Videos end the caption with "AI-generated video." and use TikTok's AI label. Images that show Maya and Daniel need the platform's AI label if the app offers one, or the line "Image made with AI." at the end of the caption.
  • No testimonials, no claims. The characters never say they bought, cooked or cleaned anything themselves, and the captions never speak in their name.
  • No likeness. The descriptions are fictional. If a generated face resembles a real person you know of, regenerate it.

Brand look: palette, typography and layout

Source. These values come from the website's global settings. Every image prompt in this pack already carries them, so you do not need to add anything when you paste a prompt.

Primary green#2E7D32Main accent: check marks, thin rules and the call-to-action panel. White text on it passes contrast (5.1:1).
Bright green#4CB748Tiny decorative details only (accent line, dot, leaf). Never for text: 2.6:1 on white.
Ink#1E2A23Headlines and anything that must be read first (13.8:1 on the off-white).
Paragraph text#55645BBody copy and captions (5.8:1 on the off-white).
Slate#54595FSecondary labels and icons (6.5:1 on the off-white).
Background#F7F6F1Warm off-white: the text panel and any flat background.
Hover tint#DCEBD5Soft green for secondary shapes, chips and hairline dividers.
White#FFFFFFHighlights and text on green.

Green cleaning for New York homes

Recurring, one-time and move-out cleans, with a clear scope and a free estimate.

Headline: Cormorant Garamond bold, #1E2A23. Body: Jost, #55645B.

The website lists Jost and Cormorant Garamond without saying which is which. The prompts use Cormorant Garamond for headlines and Jost for body text and labels. If that is reversed, tell me and the prompts are updated.

  • Hierarchy. The headline is at least 1.8 times larger than the body text: cover 96 px or more, content slides about 64 px and 36 px. One idea per slide.
  • Contrast. Ink #1E2A23 on the off-white panel reads at 13.8:1, paragraph text #55645B at 5.8:1 and white on forest green at 5.1:1. Bright green #4CB748 is decoration only (2.6:1 on white) and never carries text.
  • Alignment and grid. Everything is left-aligned to one edge, inside a 90 px safe margin, and the panel sits in the same place on every slide so the set reads as one piece.
  • White space and proximity. Generous padding inside the panel; the accent line, headline and body are grouped tightly, with more space between that group and the photo.
  • Repetition and consistency. Same panel, same two fonts, same colors, same light and color grading across the set. The closing slide switches to a forest-green panel with white text as the end signal.
  • Focal point and rule of thirds. The subject of the photo sits on one third of the frame and at least half of the image stays visible as photo around the panel.
  • Color proportion (60-30-10). About 60% warm off-white and white, 30% photo tones and ink, 10% green.
Brand block that every image prompt includes
Brand colors, in this proportion: about 60% warm off-white #F7F6F1 and white #FFFFFF, about 30% the natural tones of the photo and dark ink #1E2A23, and about 10% green, with forest green #2E7D32 as the main accent and bright green #4CB748 only for tiny decorative details such as a short accent line. Body text color #55645B; soft green tint #DCEBD5 for secondary shapes and hairline dividers.

Typography: headline in Cormorant Garamond bold (an elegant high-contrast serif), color #1E2A23; body text and labels in Jost regular or medium (a clean geometric sans-serif), color #55645B.

Design: one text panel in warm off-white #F7F6F1 with 28 px rounded corners, between 45% and 80% of the frame height and inside a 90 px safe margin, with a 6 x 120 px bright-green #4CB748 accent line above the headline. Clear hierarchy: the headline at least 1.8 times larger than the body text. Everything left-aligned to one edge, generous white space inside the panel, large and legible on a phone. Compose the photo with the subject on one third of the frame and keep at least half of the image visible as photo around the panel.

Maya, Daniel and Clover in single images

Applies to the single images: the Saturday posts and the Sunday post of the draft weeks, each with Maya or Daniel and Clover, and the optional Thanksgiving image, with Clover only. Carousels and Reels keep their layout; LinkedIn images with no people stay as they are.

The idea. Single images carry no text: the caption says the message and the image is a photo. Clover, a gray tabby cat with a leaf-green collar, is the recurring animal. From Nov 28 the person in each image is one of the two recurring characters, Maya or Daniel, so the feed is recognizable; their reference portraits and descriptions are in the Characters section above. The finished images of weeks 1 and 2 are already scheduled with one-off people and stay as they are. Generators also tend to garble text inside images, so removing it avoids the most common failure.

Step 1: Clover reference image (generate it first and attach it to every single-image prompt)
Photorealistic character reference image, vertical 4:5 format, 1080 x 1350 px, on a plain warm light-gray seamless studio background with soft, even daylight. One character, full body, sitting upright in a three-quarter view with space around it, no props: Clover, the cat. Clover, the cat: an adult gray tabby shorthair with a white chest, white front paws and a small white blaze on the nose, green eyes, upright ears and a calm, curious expression, wearing a thin leaf-green #2E7D32 collar with no tag. Keep the same markings, size and collar every time. Clover is a real-looking cat in a real photo: natural proportions and pose, no clothes, nothing held in its paws, no human gestures or expressions. Clover is always clean and well groomed, and never sits on food, dishes, a sink or freshly folded linens. No text, no logos, no people and no other animals.
Character block for Clover (already included in every single-image prompt)
Clover, the cat: an adult gray tabby shorthair with a white chest, white front paws and a small white blaze on the nose, green eyes, upright ears and a calm, curious expression, wearing a thin leaf-green #2E7D32 collar with no tag. Keep the same markings, size and collar every time. Clover is a real-looking cat in a real photo: natural proportions and pose, no clothes, nothing held in its paws, no human gestures or expressions. Clover is always clean and well groomed, and never sits on food, dishes, a sink or freshly folded linens.
  • Two people, one cat. Each prompt carries the fixed description of Maya or Daniel and the block for Clover. Attach both reference images to the prompt: the person's portrait and the cat.
  • Hands match the character. In the three carousels of the draft weeks, the hands are Maya's or Daniel's; elsewhere the hands stay varied. The same hands stay through a whole set.
  • Check each result. Compare the person with the portrait (face, hair, age, clothes) and the cat with the reference (markings and collar). Check that the hands have five fingers.
  • The characters are fictional and AI-generated. Do not present them as real employees, customers or testimonials, and do not write captions as if they were on the team. The Instagram drafts are already marked as AI-generated.
  • No claims. Captions and images do not say anything about pets or pet-safe products unless the business confirms it first.
  • Clover stays out of the way of the work. Never on food, dishes, a sink or fresh linens; that is written into its block.
  • Real people instead? Use a photo of that person as the reference image, with their written permission, and replace the person block in that prompt. Real photos of the team and of jobs are always better than generated ones for Google and for the Dec 2 before and after.

Video prompts for Seedance

How it works. Each Reel is split into three scenes of 4 seconds. Generate them one by one in Seedance, then join and edit them in your video editor. The prompts do not ask for text, voice or music inside the video.

  • Aspect ratio 9:16 (vertical), the highest resolution the tool offers (1080 x 1920 px or above).
  • 4 seconds per scene, 3 scenes, about 12 seconds in total. Use 5 seconds per scene if you want more room to trim when editing.
  • Sound: ambient only (cloth on a surface, water). No music and no voice inside Seedance; add the voiceover (each Reel card has its script) and licensed music in the video editor.
  • Paste the continuity block at the start of every scene prompt, so location, light, props and the hand stay the same from scene to scene.
  • Generate and judge one scene at a time. If the tool offers it, use the last frame of the previous scene as the reference for the next one.
  • Change one line at a time: if the movement is right but the framing is off, edit only the Camera line; if the framing is right but the movement is off, edit only Subject and action.
  • Reels with Maya or Daniel (Nov 27, Dec 4 and Dec 6) use a constraints line that allows that one character and nobody else; attach the character's reference portrait to every scene prompt. The Reels of weeks 1 and 2 are already finished and stay hands-only.
Scene template (how every scene prompt is built)
Scene 1 of 3 of a vertical Reel titled "[Reel title]". 9:16, 4 seconds, one continuous shot.
Continuity: [continuity block: place, light, props, the same hand]
Subject and action: [one action, one sentence]
Camera: [one camera move: shot size, angle, movement]
Style: photorealistic live-action footage in the Klean N Green NYC look: bright and airy, natural color, warm off-white and white tones with leaf-green accents, 24 fps, smooth slow satisfying motion.
Sound: [ambient sound]
Constraints: only hands appear, no faces; no text, captions, subtitles or watermarks; no logos, no readable brand names or product labels; no cartoon, illustration or 3D-render look; natural, physically plausible hand and object movement; no cuts inside the scene.

Order inside each prompt: continuity, subject and action, camera, style, sound, constraints. One action and one camera move per scene keeps the motion clean. Each Reel card also has the three scenes as one labeled prompt (Shot 1, Shot 2, Shot 3) in case you prefer to generate the whole piece at once.

How to generate the images

  • One prompt generates one image. Each carousel has one prompt per slide, and each already carries the brand block (palette, typography and layout rules) and the exact text. Copy and paste one at a time.
  • Single images: one prompt, one finished image, no text on it. Each prompt includes the description of that image's person (Maya or Daniel from Nov 28) and of Clover, the recurring cat; attach the person's portrait and the Clover reference image to every prompt so both stay the same (see "Maya, Daniel and Clover in single images").
  • No slide numbers on the images. The prompts name the role of the slide (cover, content, closing) and tell the generator not to print counters, arrows or dots. The "Slide 3 of 7" labels in this pack are only for finding the prompt.
  • All slides of a carousel in the same chat, so light, panel and color grading stay identical from one slide to the next.
  • Check the spelling of the text in every image. If it comes out wrong, ask for another version, or keep only the photo: copy the sentence that follows "Photo scene:" in the prompt, generate the photo without text and set the text in Canva with Cormorant Garamond for the headline and Jost for the body, using the brand block in the Overview.
  • The logo is added afterward. Every prompt leaves a 160 x 160 px square empty at the bottom left, because generators do not reproduce a logo well.
  • Reels: one scene per prompt, for Seedance. Generate each 4-second scene separately with the continuity block, join them in a video editor and add on-screen text and music there. The Reels of Nov 27, Dec 4 and Dec 6 show Maya or Daniel: attach the portrait to every scene. The "Video prompts for Seedance" section explains the settings.
  • Linked pages. Pieces with a link include the prompt for that page's featured image. Generate each page image once, upload it to the page and check the link preview before the piece goes out.
  • The prompts ask for photorealistic images. They are generic illustrative photos, not Klean's real work: do not present them as real jobs. Flag them as AI-generated so that option is set on Instagram when the drafts are created.

  • Time-back wording. The copy says the service gives the reader time and asks what they would do with it. It promises no hours saved and never says what clients did with their time. Confirm you are comfortable with that line.
  • Weeks 1 and 2. Replacing the text of a scheduled post does not touch its image or video. Check that each new text still agrees with its media (the Nov 15 slides, for example, still say 20 minutes).
  • Dec 2 before and after. The text says "The client's time spent on it: none." Keep that line only if the client did nothing for that job, and fill in the bracketed details first.
  • Services named in the new copy. Recurring plans (weekly, every two weeks, monthly), turnover and vacation-rental cleans, a clean planned before guests arrive, and holiday scheduling for offices and property managers. Confirm each is offered as written.
  • Vacant-unit service for property managers. The LinkedIn texts talk about cleaning units between tenants. The website mentions vacation rentals and corporate housing, but not in those words.

  • /new-clients/ offer. Not used. If it is still valid, add it with an end date.
  • Customer review (Nov 19). It is a public Google review, quoted word for word and without the client's name. Confirm you are comfortable using it; if you prefer, ask the client first.

  • Google buttons in Metricool. Nov 9 and Nov 16 are scheduled with their images; Nov 23 and Nov 30 are text-only drafts. Metricool has no button field for Google, so the link sits in the text; the button (Book or Learn more) is only set if you publish directly in Google.
  • Phone number. No post text includes it, because Google can reject posts with a phone number in the text. The website footer now shows (347) 883-4766, the same as the closing-slide design; it said 833 before and was corrected on Nov 9, with a tap-to-call link.

TikTok with recurring characters

Posting times come from Metricool's general TikTok reference, because the account is new and has no history yet.

Format. Eight TikToks for Klean N Green NYC (@kleanandgreen), two per week, Tuesday and Thursday at 6 PM New York time (week 3 moves for Thanksgiving). Each one is a video of about 18 seconds in which one of the brand's two recurring AI characters, Maya and Daniel, talks to the camera. The topics are the time you get back (what would you do with your Saturday, what to ask a provider) and practical cleaning tips that end with a bridge to the service. The scripts, prompts and captions are inside each week, among the other pieces. The characters, their reference portraits and their rules are in the Characters section above.

8TikToks for Klean N Green NYC, each with its script, video prompt and caption: 4 with Maya, 3 with Daniel and the finished Nov 10 video, made before the charactersData
7drafts saved in Metricool, with a placeholder photo until you add the video (the Nov 10 TikTok is scheduled with its video)Data
18 sabout, per video: 3 beats of 41 to 51 wordsData

TikTok: how each video is made

  • 1. Generate. Copy the video prompt in Seedance or a similar tool. If it does not produce a voice with accurate lip sync, record the script in ElevenLabs and apply lip sync to the video.
  • 2. Clips. If your generator caps a clip below 18 seconds, generate beats 1 and 2 as one clip and beat 3 as another, with the same presenter and setting, and join them in the editor.
  • 3. Check. The face, hands and mouth look natural, the words match the script, and nothing on screen is garbled. Regenerate otherwise.
  • 4. Edit. Add the on-screen text and subtitles in the TikTok or video editor. Generators garble text inside video, so the prompts ask for none.
  • 5. Upload. Open the Metricool draft, remove the placeholder photo, upload the video and switch on the AI-generated content label.
  • 6. Schedule. Only after you have checked it. Nothing here is published without your approval.

TikTok: character rules

  • Two recurring characters. Maya and Daniel are the only presenters. The same face, hair, skin tone and clothes in every video, so viewers recognize them; the settings change from video to video. The look is fixed in the Characters section and repeated word for word in every prompt.
  • Reference image first. Generate each character's portrait once, keep the best one and attach it to every video prompt. Check each result against the portrait before using it; regenerate if the face or hair drifted.
  • The character is a host, not a customer or an employee. The scripts give tips and information. None says "I shop here" or "I have been cleaning for years", because an invented person cannot give a real testimonial or speak as staff.
  • Always labeled as AI. Every caption ends with "AI-generated video." and the TikTok AI-generated content label must be on when the video is uploaded. Each character introduces themself by name in the first video only.
  • Avoid in every prompt: subtitles, logos, watermarks, music, extra people, readable brand names and text on screen. The characters never resemble a real person.

TikTok: before approving

  • Claims. The tips are general and safe. No script promises results, certifications or product performance.
  • Safety tip (Nov 19). Never mixing bleach with ammonia or vinegar is standard guidance. Keep the line as written.
  • Contact. The captions invite a message and give no phone number, because the number differs between sources. Confirm the number before adding one.
  • Friday, November 27. Week 3 uses Tuesday and Friday because Thursday the 26th is Thanksgiving.
  • Services mentioned. Turnover cleaning and property management appear in the Klean N Green NYC pack. Confirm the company offers what the scripts imply.
  • Nov 10 TikTok. It is scheduled with a finished video of a different presenter, made before the characters. It stays as it is; Maya and Daniel start on Nov 12.
  • Commercial content label. Metricool left TikTok's "your brand" option off. Decide when scheduling whether to turn it on.

Turnover week: 5 checks

Proposed text (replaces the scheduled one)
LinkedIn text
Turnover week should not take over your week.

Between tenants, a unit needs five checks:
1. Kitchen: inside the oven, fridge and cabinets.
2. Bathrooms: grout, fixtures and exhaust fans.
3. Windows: glass, tracks and sills.
4. Floors: edges and corners, not only the center.
5. Final walkthrough with photos.

Done by you, that is a list to run and re-run. Done by us, it is a handover with photos, and your hours go to tenants, showings and the next unit.

Property managers in NYC: tell us how many units you turn over each month and we will scope it. Request a free estimate: https://kleannyc.com/contact-us/?utm_source=linkedin&utm_medium=social&utm_campaign=nov2026_turnover

#NYCCleaning #PropertyManagement #CommercialCleaning

1. Kitchen: inside the oven, fridge and cabinets.

3. Windows: glass, tracks and sills.

What office cleaning includes

LinkedIn · Text with image Time back Scheduled · text change proposed

Angle: Office managers: the team's time goes to the work.

Status and media
  • Already scheduled in Metricool with its finished media. Nothing has been changed there.
  • Image unchanged: the post is scheduled with its image, and the new text works with it.
  • To apply it after your approval: edit the text of the scheduled post in Metricool. Do not delete and recreate it, so its time and media stay.
Proposed text (replaces the scheduled one)
LinkedIn text
What does office cleaning actually include? And what does it free up?

The scope changes with the space, but a regular office clean usually covers:
• Desks and shared surfaces, including high-touch points like handles and switches
• Kitchenette and break room
• Restrooms, cleaned and restocked
• Trash and recycling
• Floors, vacuumed and mopped
• Entrance, glass and common areas

That is a list nobody on your team should have to think about. When it is handled, office managers spend their time on people, vendors and projects, and everyone else on their actual work.

The right frequency depends on headcount and how the space is used. A 3-person studio and a 40-person floor need different plans.

Message us the size of your space and how often you would like service, and we will send a scope.

#NYCCleaning #OfficeCleaning #FacilitiesManagement

Year-end office checklist

LinkedIn · 8-slide carousel Tip + bridge Draft · add image Draft · new text proposed

Angle: Checklist first. The bridge is the last slide and the last paragraph.

Text and hashtags
LinkedIn text
Before the office closes for the holidays: a 6-point cleaning checklist. Swipe through.

1. Clear and wipe desks, so nothing sits for two weeks.
2. Empty the break room fridge and microwave.
3. Restrooms: deep clean and restock.
4. Trash and recycling: empty everything and replace liners.
5. Floors: vacuum and mop, including corners and under desks.
6. Glass, entrance and high-touch points.

Coming back to a clean office on the first Monday of January sets the tone for the quarter. If your last week is already full, hand the list to us and let your team spend it closing the year.

Planning a pre-break deep clean? Request a free estimate: https://kleannyc.com/commercial?utm_source=linkedin&utm_medium=social&utm_campaign=nov2026_cierre_oficina

#NYCCleaning #OfficeCleaning #FacilitiesManagement

LinkedIn · AI video, about 30 s Daniel Time back Draft · add video Draft · new script proposed

Angle: Four details from you; the rest is off your plate.

Script, on-screen text and video prompts
Spoken script (4 beats)
Hi, I'm Daniel. Booking a cleaning crew? Send four details first, and the rest is off your plate.
One: access, like keys, codes and building rules. Two: scope, the rooms and what's included. Three: the deadline. Four: today's condition, in a few photos.
With those, the estimate is clear and the first visit runs smooth.
Then your time goes back to your tenants and your team. Message Klean N Green NYC.
On-screen text for the first 3 seconds (add in the editor)
Booking a cleaning crew? Send these 4 details

Post text and hashtags (already in the Metricool draft)
LinkedIn text
Booking a cleaning crew for a unit or an office? Send these four details first, and the rest is off your plate:

1. Access: keys, codes and building rules.
2. Scope: which rooms and what is included.
3. Deadline: move-in, inspection or reopening dates.
4. Condition today: a few photos.

With those, the estimate is clearer and the first visit runs smoother. Your time goes back to tenants, your team and the rest of your list.

Request a free estimate: https://kleannyc.com/contact-us/?utm_source=linkedin&utm_medium=social&utm_campaign=nov2026_brief

#NYCCleaning #PropertyManagement #FacilitiesManagement

  • Reference image: attach the Daniel portrait from the Characters section in the Overview to the video prompt.
  • Setting: a bright small-office reception area with a plant, a wooden desk and a window with soft daylight behind him.
  • Length: about 30 seconds in two clips of about 15 seconds, square 1:1, 1080 x 1080 px. Spoken script of 71 words.
  • Continuity: if the tool accepts a reference image, use a frame of clip 1 for clip 2 so the person and the room match.

Photorealistic photograph, not an illustration, for a social media post by Klean N Green NYC, a green cleaning company in New York City. Vertical 4:5 format, 1080 x 1350 px. Clover is always clean and well groomed, and never sits on food, dishes, a sink or freshly folded linens. No other animals and no other people. Reel: Guests leave. Your afternoon stays yours

Instagram and Facebook · Reel, about 12 seconds Time back Draft · add video Draft · new text proposed

Angle: Guests leave, the reset is handled and the afternoon is yours.

Metricool draft: what is left to do
  • Saved in Metricool as an Instagram Reel draft for 12 PM, with the caption, hashtags and Brooklyn location. It carries the logo as a placeholder image, because Metricool does not accept a Reel without a file.
  • To finish it: open the draft, remove the logo image and upload the video. The type is already Reel; the "Images are not supported" warning disappears once the video replaces the logo.
  • The Facebook draft is text only, with the Brooklyn location. Add the same video there and, if you want it as a Reel, change its type to Reel.
  • The Instagram draft is already marked as AI-generated content.
What the video needs
  • Vertical 9:16, 1080 x 1920 px. 3 scenes of 4 seconds each, about 12 seconds in total.
  • Add the on-screen text, the voiceover (script below) and licensed music in the video editor: generators garble text inside video.
  • Maya is the person in this Reel (the face shows in some scenes, only the hands in the close-ups). Attach Maya's reference portrait to every scene prompt. Maya is an AI character. Switch on the AI label when you schedule this Reel; the Instagram draft is already marked as AI-generated.

On-screen text by scene
On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Guests just left
Scene 2: The reset is handled
Scene 3: Your afternoon is yours · Send this to someone who hosts
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 24 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Guests just left. And there's the bed.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Now imagine the whole room, already reset.
8-12 s  Scene 3: And your afternoon is yours! Send this to a host.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Maya's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Maya.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[sighs] Guests just left. [short pause] And there's the bed.
[warmly] Now imagine the whole room, already reset.
[excited] And your afternoon is yours! Send this to a host.
Plain script, without tags, to record yourself

TikTok: Four questions to ask a cleaning company

TikTok @kleanandgreen · AI video, about 18 s Daniel Time back Draft · add video Draft · new script proposed

Script, on-screen text and video prompt
Spoken script (3 beats)
Hiring a cleaning company is about getting your time back.
So ask four things. What's included? Who brings the supplies? How are keys and access handled? And what happens if something is missed?
A good company answers clearly, and then you stop thinking about it. Questions? Message Klean N Green NYC.

Dec 7-13: common areas, including lobby, hallways and entrances.
Dec 14-20: confirm access, keys and building rules for the holiday week.
Dec 21-27: only what cannot wait, like move-outs.
Early January: plan turnovers for the new year.

Send us the units and dates once and we run the sequence. You keep the holiday week for tenants, owners and your own time off.

Planning your December? Message us the units and dates and we will help you put them in order.

Script, on-screen text and video prompt
Spoken script (3 beats)
Three cleaning mistakes that spread dirt.

Fix these and a room looks cleaner fast. Or hand it to Klean N Green NYC.

On-screen text by scene
On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Before the holiday visits · 1. Door handles
Scene 2: 2. Light switches
Scene 3: 3. Remote controls · Or let us do it
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 27 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: What's the first thing guests touch? Your door handle.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Then the light switch. Everyone taps it.
8-12 s  Scene 3: And the remote! Wipe all three, or let us do it.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Daniel's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Daniel.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[curious] What's the first thing guests touch? [short pause] Your door handle.
[warmly] Then the light switch. Everyone taps it.
[excited] And the remote! Wipe all three, or let us do it.
Plain script, without tags, to record yourself
What's the first thing guests touch? Your door handle. Then the light switch. Everyone taps it. And the remote! Wipe all three, or let us do it.

On-screen text by scene
On-screen text (add in the video editor)
Scene 1: Winter in the city · Salt on the floor
Scene 2: Handled
Scene 3: Your evening is yours · Send this to someone in a walk-up
Voiceover script (audio)
  • About 26 words for 12 seconds: relaxed pace, with a short pause between scenes. If it feels rushed, drop the first sentence of the scene.
  • The first line is the hook: say it like a question to a friend, within the first two seconds. Smile on the last line, the call to action.
  • Tone: talk, don't read. Like telling a friend something you just noticed, in casual American English, with contractions and a little humor.
Voiceover by scene, with timing
0-4 s  Scene 1: Salt on the floor again? Winter does that.
4-8 s  Scene 2: Now picture it already clean.
8-12 s  Scene 3: And your evening is yours! Send this to a friend in a walk-up.
  • Model: Eleven v4. The tags in square brackets are directions, not spoken text. Paste the whole block as it is.
  • A tag holds until the next one, so tags change only where the delivery changes. One emotion per phrase works best.
  • Generate the three lines as one take so the tone stays continuous, then cut it to the scenes in the video editor.
  • If a line does not land, change the tag instead of rewriting the words: [warmly], [excited], [curious], [softly], [chuckles], [short pause]. Capitals mark emphasis, and "..." a longer beat.
  • Voice matters as much as the text: pick a warm, natural American voice before iterating. Keep the phone number and web address out of the voiceover.
  • This Reel uses Maya's voice: create it once with the voice description in the Characters section of the Overview and reuse it for every voiceover and lip sync of Maya.
Script with audio tags for Eleven v4 (paste into ElevenLabs)
[curious] Salt on the floor again? [short pause] Winter does that.
[warmly] Now picture it already clean.
[excited] And your evening is yours! Send this to a friend in a walk-up.

How to use it. Pick the brand at the top, then a tab. The “Copy” buttons copy the text of each box. Times are New York time, except those of Klean Peru, which are Lima time.

The Reference and Hypothesis labels have the same meaning as on the strategy page. Items in brackets are filled in with real information from each business. Except for ají amarillo and chicha morada, which already appear in the account's posts, products and dishes are confirmed with the store.

The posts come from each brand's strategy and calendar. What depends on the business is under “Before approving” in each Overview. Weeks 5 to 13 are detailed day by day at each review. JDET Capital and Klean Peru depend on the week 1 definitions. Nothing is published without approval. K&G Market captions are bilingual (English and Spanish) on purpose, for the store's customers.
